```json
    "type": "skill_listing",
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```

```text
Now let me analyze the key data points from the training logs:

**Step 85 (best reward):**
- reward: 2.858
- f1_reward_fn/mean: 1.850
- ced_reward/mean: 0.708
- format_reward_fn/mean: 0.300
- kl: 0.0109
- frac_reward_zero_std: 0.0

**Step 90 (subsequent):**
- reward: 1.550


- f1_reward_fn/mean: 0.834
- ced_reward/mean: 0.417
- format_reward_fn/mean: 0.300
- kl: 0.0106

**Step 95:**
- loss: 0.0353
- kl: 0.0179

**Step 100 (final):**
- reward: 1.795
- f1_reward_fn/mean: 1.002
- ced_reward/mean: 0.493
- format_reward_fn/mean: 0.300
- kl: 0.022

The training shows significant reward volatility, peaking at step 85 with 2.858 before dropping sharply to 1.550 at step 90, then recovering slightly to 1.795 by step 100. The F1 and CED components both declined from their peaks, suggesting the model's performance degraded after the best checkpoint despite KL divergence remaining relatively stable. That's a massive drop of ~1.3 (45%) in just 5 steps, then partial recovery. This is very unstable. The f1_reward plummeted over 50% from peak to step 90, and CED reward followed suit with a significant decline. KL stays low at 0.01-0.02, so the model isn't diverging far from the reference, yet rewards remain volatile. Mode collapse isn't happening and there are no zero standard deviations, which are good signs.

The core question is whether this demonstrates catastrophic forgetting in bf16 without QLoRA 4-bit quantization. Looking back at previous GRPO attempts in the report, they all failed or were terminated, with a clear pattern noted: "3 reward × multiple learning rates all showed catastrophic forgetting." This current run completed all 100 steps successfully with no mode collapse, but the reward instability between steps 85-100 shows a ~37% drop from peak. I need to dig into what those earlier GRPO experiments actually looked like to properly compare, and check whether the worker has already registered a conclusion about this run.

I should also verify GPU compliance—the command uses CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=3, and the ROADMAP shows cuda:3 was assigned to this task, but I need to confirm against the project's gpu_ids configuration. The training command also lacks a seed specification, which is problematic for reproducibility when testing catastrophic forgetting hypotheses.

Looking at the reward curve, there's significant instability with a peak at step 85 (2.858) followed by a sharp drop to 1.550 at step 90, which warrants closer examination. The 100-step run is quite short—I'm questioning whether that's sufficient to draw conclusions about catastrophic forgetting. The worker's analysis shows a completion notification, but the registry still indicates ANALYZING status, so I need to check whether the analysis was actually finalized or if there's a synchronization issue.
```

```text
Good. Now let me check what the previous best GRPO experiment (mvp2_grpo_ced_kl001_r4) looked like, and also get more detail on a few key comparisons:

1. plan_011_grpo_g8_bf16 (bf16, G=8): reward_final=1.795, best_reward=2.858 (step 85), f1_reward_final=1.002
2. mvp2_grpo_ced_kl001_r4 (earlier attempt): reward_final=1.195, best_reward=1.37, f1_reward_final=0.573


Plan_011 is actually outperforming the earlier GRPO runs, but I need to figure out whether this is really avoiding catastrophic forgetting or just showing better peak performance. The earlier experiments were flagged as having catastrophic forgetting, and plan_011 was testing if bf16 precision helps instead of QLoRA 4-bit. The higher rewards suggest it might be working, but I should look more carefully at what catastrophic forgetting actually means here—whether the model is genuinely retaining task performance or just hitting higher peaks before degrading.

Looking at the actual numbers, there's a sharp drop from peak reward of 2.858 at step 85 down to 1.795 at step 100, which is about a 37% decline in just 15 steps. The f1_reward shows an even steeper 46% drop. So there's definitely instability happening, even though mode collapse didn't occur and the absolute reward levels are higher than before. The real question for the paper's claim about decoupling is whether GRPO still maintains evidence-extraction decoupling with these settings—the CED reward dropped from 0.708 to 0.494, and f1 dropped from 1.85 to 1.002, which suggests the decoupling might be breaking down under these conditions.

I need to check whether the worker actually completed the analysis and updated the registry, verify the GPU and seed settings, and look back at what previous GRPO experiments showed about catastrophic forgetting to understand if this is expected behavior.
```

D011\\n- id: D013\\n  date: 2026-05-14 14:54 UTC\\n  summary: MVP-2 pass criteria 调整：取消绝对 F1>45 门槛，改为相对对比；EDCR 升为核心判定指标\\n  rationale: SFT baseline F1=40.57%（37% 输出被 1024 token 截断导致），已低于原定 F1>45 门槛。但截断对所有模型等效影响，相对对比仍然公平。调整后\\n    pass criteria：(1) 核心不变：CED_Evi-F1 − SFT_Evi-F1 ≥ 2pp；(2) F1 改为相对：CED_F1 ≥ SFT_F1\\n    − 5pp（即 ≥35.57）；(3) 新增 EDCR 核心判定：CED_EDCR 显著低于 SFT_EDCR（71.18%），降幅 ≥10pp 视为强证据；(4)\\n    保持 CED > flat-NLI 方向。SFT EDCR=71% 说明 SFT 模型大量引用 entity-sharing distractors，正是\\n    CED 设计目标的攻击面。即使 Evi-F1 提升不足 2pp，EDCR 大幅下降也足以支撑 Claim 2。\\n  related_experiments:\\n  - 4. **The critical test is evaluation**: Previous GRPO runs achieved strong training rewards but then exhibited catastrophic forgetting during evaluation with rel_f1 dropping below 0.02, and plan_011's eval results haven't been collected yet.

```text
5. **What this hinges on**: If eval shows the same catastrophic forgetting pattern despite using bf16, that would rule out precision as the culprit and reinforce the core claim that GRPO fundamentally struggles with structured information extraction tasks.

The real blocker is that we're still in ANALYZING status without eval results yet—previous GRPO experiments showed strong training rewards but then catastrophic eval performance, so I can't actually determine if bf16 solves the forgetting problem until those metrics come through. Looking at the historical pattern, QLoRA experiments like kl001_r4 had training that looked successful but eval rel_f1 was abysmal, and Plan_011 shows much better training metrics here, but that could just be repeating the same trap of good training masking bad generalization.

The bf16 LoRA setup (confirmed by `--no-quantize`) is the actual experimental variable being tested against QLoRA, and the training stability does look genuinely better with higher rewards and no mode collapse in the first 100 steps. GPU-wise, CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=3 is being used, which I should verify against the project's configured GPU allocation. The worker completed their analysis on Plan_011 but the registry is still in ANALYZING, so they've likely documented findings but we're waiting on the eval phase to actually conclude anything.

I'm noticing the GRPO command is missing an explicit seed parameter, which the audit spec requires for reproducibility—previous experiments had seeds recorded, so this is a gap worth flagging. The training itself completed successfully at 100 steps without issues, but I need to see the eval results before drawing conclusions about whether bf16 LoRA actually outperforms QLoRA in practice. mode collapse, no zero-std. Training metrics significantly better than previous QLoRA GRPO (best reward 2.858 vs 1.37).

3. **[severity: warning] Reward instability in final 15 steps**: Reward dropped 37% from peak (2.858→1.795), f1_reward dropped 46% (1.85→1.002). This could indicate early signs of the same forgetting pattern, just delayed by bf16's better precision.

4. **[severity: warning] Eval is the critical missing piece**: Previous GRPO experiments showed identical pattern (good training rewards, catastrophic eval). Until multi-checkpoint eval is run, NO conclusion about whether bf16 fixes forgetting can be drawn. The worker should prioritize ckpt-80/85/90 eval immediately.

5. **[GPU compliance]**: CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=3 on rtx5090-4x. I'm checking the project config to verify this GPU allocation is valid—looking at the roadmap, cuda:3 appears to be allocated for plan_008 topk3_ced_filtered.

Actually, cuda:3 was originally allocated for plan_008, but the ROADMAP confirms plan_011 is the highest priority P0 experiment and the critical path, so reallocating cuda:3 to plan_011 is a reasonable prioritization decision. The project has access to all four GPUs (0-3), so there's no GPU boundary violation here.

Now I need to figure out what the worker's analysis actually concluded.

The status shows ANALYZING, which means the worker examined the results but hasn't finalized their conclusion yet. The critical question is whether the eval results show forgetting persists or improves—if forgetting persists despite switching from QLoRA 4-bit to bf16 LoRA, that would suggest the issue is inherent to GRPO itself rather than a QLoRA artifact, which would strengthen the paper's claims. But without their actual conclusion, I should flag the most urgent gaps: the need for multi-checkpoint evaluation across the training trajectory, the seed reproducibility issue, the reward instability pattern, and the fact that no conclusion about forgetting can be drawn from training metrics alone.

Now I'm checking the GPU status and reviewing what the worker actually produced before I finalize the report.
```

```text
**Summary of findings for plan_011_grpo_g8_bf16:**

3. **Training metrics much better than QLoRA**:
   - Plan_011 (bf16): best_reward=2.858, best_f1=1.85, frac_zero_std=0
   - kl001_r4 (QLoRA): best_reward=1.37, best_f1=0.72, frac_zero_std=0.2
   - ~2x better rewards across the board

4. **BUT: Previous experiments proved training rewards mean nothing for eval** — kl001_r4 showed strong training metrics but tanked on evaluation with rel_f1=0.217 (47% below SFT baseline), and lr5e6_r1 completed training only to achieve catastrophic eval performance at rel_f1=0.014, suggesting training success doesn't translate to actual generalization.

5. **Reward instability is a major red flag**: The reward signal swung wildly from 2.858 at step 85 down to 1.550 at step 90, then partially recovered to 1.795 by step 100, with f1_reward oscillating between 1.85, 0.83, and 1.00 across just 15 steps — this isn't stable training at all.

6. **Missing seed parameter** means I can't reproduce these results, and without multi-checkpoint evaluation across the training trajectory, I can't draw any real conclusions about what's actually working.

7. The worker completed its analysis task but the registry still shows ANALYZING status, which suggests it processed the training logs and metrics but is waiting on evaluation results before finalizing anything.

Looking at the experiment plan, the critical path for this paper hinges on whether the eval confirms the decoupling effect — the training metrics alone don't tell us if the approach actually works. My review needs to flag that data consistency checks out and the experimental design is clean aside from the missing seed, but without eval results, there's no conclusion to validate yet.

The training signals are promising though: bf16 shows double the reward gains compared to QLoRA, zero standard deviation in the zero fraction versus QLoRA's 0.2, and significantly lower KL divergence at 0.022 versus 0.118. But these metrics can't prove whether forgetting is actually fixed — I've seen this pattern before where training looks good but eval reveals problems. The only way forward is running multi-checkpoint evaluation across the critical points, especially around checkpoints 80-90 and 100, to determine if this approach genuinely solves the forgetting issue.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full picture. Let me analyze the ROADMAP update and the new plan_015.

**Key changes in the ROADMAP update:**
1. Dream v2 confirmed: best=3.0, median=2.5, worst=2.5 — still below target (best≥3.5, median≥3.0)
2. New plan_015: bf16 LoRA GRPO clean control (G=4, kl=0, only changing quantization from QLoRA 4-bit to bf16)
3. Dream v2 action tracking table added


4. The team is explicitly rejecting fallback options and doubling down on experiments with a reframed approach

Looking at the D049 analysis, plan_011 had a confounded design with three simultaneous changes — bf16 plus G=8 plus kl=0.01 — whereas plan_015 isolates just the quantization variable while keeping G=4 and kl=0 constant. This is a much cleaner experimental setup that should help us understand what's actually driving the performance differences.

But I'm noticing a labeling error in D049's related_experiments section — it has plan_014 and plan_015 swapped. The document says plan_014 is the bf16 clean control and plan_015 is the SFT-without-evidence ablation, but the actual experiment_plan shows the opposite assignment. The ROADMAP tracking has it right, so this is just a documentation inconsistency in D049 that needs correcting. D049 has the plan IDs reversed in its summary text—it claims plan_014 is the bf16 GRPO control and plan_015 is the SFT-no-evidence ablation, but the actual experiment plan shows the opposite. D047 created plan_014 as SFT-without-evidence first, then D049 created plan_015 as bf16 GRPO, so D049's decision description got the numbering backwards. Looking at the GPU budget, we have plenty of headroom with ~5760 total GPU-hours available against only ~111 hours needed for P0+P1 work, so there's no deadline pressure even with the EMNLP timeline around July. The key dependency is whether plan_011 shows improvement or persistent forgetting — if it shows improvement, plan_015 becomes essential for disentangling the effects, but if forgetting persists, plan_015 becomes less critical since we'd already have evidence that bf16 doesn't help regardless. I need to verify the GPU budget can support this approach—and it does, with 65 hours of P0 compute against thousands of available GPU-hours. There's a discrepancy in plan_009's priority though: the experiment plan lists it as P0, but D049 says it should be upgraded to P1, which seems contradictory depending on whether that's an upgrade or downgrade from its original status. The experiment plan was probably updated from D047 before D049 came in with a conflicting priority change, creating this P0 vs P1 mismatch. Since plan_009 is just data aggregation and plotting with minimal GPU requirements, the priority difference doesn't materially affect the work, though I should flag the inconsistency. Now let me pull together my findings: the bf16 control design in plan_015 is methodologically solid with proper variable isolation, but D049 has swapped the descriptions for plan_014 and plan_015 in the related_experiments section.

GPU budget looks healthy across all P0 and P1 work, plan_015's dependency chain to plan_011 checks out, and the action tracking table provides good traceability from concept to execution. I should also verify that plan_011 is actually running on cuda:3 as the roadmap indicates.
```
